# 项目: phase-gated-compound-sparse

## 运行环境

- SSH 连接命令: ssh connect.westd.seetacloud.com  # 必须用此命令，~/.ssh/config 自动应用密钥和跳板机，禁止用裸 IP
- 项目代码根目录: /root/autodl-tmp/pacs


## 环境搭建

## 输出风格

## 任务：论文全文数字一致性审查

你需要交叉验证论文中所有定量数据是否与服务器上的 canonical 数据源一致。

### 环境
- 服务器：westd-22162
- SSH 激活：`source /root/miniconda3/etc/profile.d/conda.sh && conda activate base`
- 数据目录：`/root/autodl-tmp/pacs/analysis/`
- 论文文件（本地）：`/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_11/projects/phase-gated-compound-sparse/artifacts/_project/paper/`

### 步骤

1. **读取所有论文 .tex 文件**（本地 Read 工具）：
   - abstract.tex, introduction.tex, results.tex, analysis.tex, discussion.tex, conclusion.tex
   - background.tex, methodology.tex
   - appendix.tex, appendix_results.tex, appendix_disc_analysis.tex

2. **SSH 读取 canonical 数据文件**（ssh_read_only）：
   - `experiment_summary_table.json` — median_diff, II values by phase/strategy/sparsity
   - `cohens_d_bf16.json` — DeepSeek paired d_z and bootstrap CIs
   - `cohens_d_bf16_qwen.json` — Qwen paired d_z and bootstrap CIs
   - `cross_model_analysis.json` — Spearman rho, gradient permutation p, per-problem concordance
   - `permutation_test_results.json` — permutation test p-values, observed differences, CIs
   - 如果有 `discrimination_metrics.json` 或类似文件也读取（Gini, CV deltas）
   - 先 `ls /root/autodl-tmp/pacs/analysis/` 看有什么文件

3. **逐个数字交叉验证**：
   对每个 .tex 文件中的每个数字，检查是否与 canonical 数据匹配。重点检查：
   - Table 1 (tab:aggregate-ii): median_diff 值 0.39/1.48/7.16/14.59 (importance) 和 0.01/-0.02/0.54/1.54 (random)
   - Severity gradient G: 69/103/222/274
   - Crossover numbers: 0.032 vs 0.818 (high), 7.196 vs 1.363 (low)
   - Cohen's d_z: DS 0.44/0.52/0.68/0.80, QW 0.28/0.55/0.89/1.02
   - Permutation test: observed diffs 0.37/0.97/3.34/9.85, CIs, p-values
   - Cross-model: Qwen Delta 0.04/0.23/1.27/2.94
   - Discrimination metrics: Gini deltas, CV deltas
   - Entropy deltas: DS -0.040/-0.047/-0.045/-0.044, QW -0.073/-0.076/+0.045/-0.007
   - Bootstrap CIs in Table 5 (tab:cohens-d)
   - Random eviction Qwen s030: p=0.012, d_z=-0.04

4. **产出报告**：
   - 写一份审查报告到本地：`/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_11/projects/phase-gated-compound-sparse/artifacts/numerical_consistency_audit.md`
   - 报告格式：每个被检查的数字列出 [文件:行号] [论文中的值] [数据源中的值] [MATCH/MISMATCH]
   - 在报告顶部给出总结：总共检查了多少个数字，多少匹配，多少不匹配
   - 不匹配的数字必须标红并给出修正建议

### 硬约束
- 所有数字必须追溯到 canonical 数据文件，不允许"看起来对"就通过
- Cohen's d 必须是 paired d_z（不是 unpaired）
- II 必须是 median（不是 mean）
- 数据源必须是 bf16（不是 fp16）
- 注意 segment-level median_diff vs per-problem paired median_diff 的区别（论文主文用 segment-level，Cohen's d 表用 per-problem paired）

```text
1	\section{Discrimination Collapse Details}
2	\label{app:discrimination-collapse-details}
3	
4	This cross-model consistency (Table~\ref{tab:discrimination-metrics-full}) establishes discrimination collapse as a robust consequence of compound sparsity. When importance scores lose discriminative power, thresholding the bottom-$s$ fraction becomes noise-sensitive: small perturbations change which positions cross the eviction boundary. In the limit of perfectly uniform scores, importance-based eviction degenerates into an arbitrary selection functionally equivalent to biased random eviction. The same pattern holds under random eviction (DeepSeek: $-0.031$ to $-0.073$; Qwen: $-0.019$ to $-0.045$), confirming that discrimination collapse is a consequence of head pruning \emph{per se}, not of the eviction strategy. % exp_id: c4_entropy_deepseek, c4_entropy_qwen
5	
6	
7	\section{Extended Discussion}
8	\label{app:extended-discussion}
9	
10	\paragraph{On the predictability of phase-modulated severity.}
11	The magnitude of the severity gradient---$69$--$274{\times}$ amplification---far exceeds what a naive ``less discriminative $\Rightarrow$ somewhat worse'' argument would predict. % exp_id: bf16_importance_s015..s030; source: experiment_summary_table.json; G=low/high=69,103,222,274.
12	The compound interaction is not uniformly fragile---it is selectively fragile in a phase-dependent manner ($\widetilde{\mathrm{II}}_{\text{high}} < 0.06$ at $s \leq 0.30$; at $s = 0.35$, high-phase II rises to $0.105$ in a supplementary experiment, indicating onset of compound interaction even in high-sparsity phases at extreme pruning rates). % exp_id: bf16_importance_s015..s030, bf16_ds_imp_s035; high-phase medians: 0.006, 0.015, 0.032, 0.053; s035 II_high=0.1051
13	
14	\paragraph{Random masking as falsification control.}
15	If compound severity amplification were an inherent property of applying two compression methods jointly, random eviction should exhibit a comparable phase gradient. It does not: random eviction fails 7/8 paired permutation tests across both models; the single exception (Qwen $s{=}0.30$, $p{=}0.012$) exhibits negligible effect size ($d_z{=}{-}0.04$). The absence of a phase gradient under random masking isolates the amplification as strategy-specific. % exp_id: bf16_ds_rand_s015..s030, bf16_qw_rand_s015..s030; verdict: 7/8 FAIL, 1 PASS (qw_rand_s030 p=0.012 d=-0.035 negligible)
16	
17	\paragraph{Cross-model magnitude asymmetry.}
18	Qwen's median severity differences ($0.04 \to 0.23 \to 1.27 \to 2.94$) are approximately one-fifth of DeepSeek's ($0.39 \to 1.48 \to 7.16 \to 14.59$), and the onset threshold differs: DeepSeek's gradient is significant from $s{=}0.15$ ($d_z{=}0.44$), while Qwen's becomes significant only at $s \geq 0.20$ ($d_z{=}0.55$). % exp_id: cohens_d_bf16.json, cohens_d_bf16_qwen.json
19	We conjecture that distillation amplifies the phase-strategy interaction by producing sharper internal phase transitions---a hypothesis testable by comparing distilled and non-distilled variants at matched scale.
20	
21	\paragraph{Architecture generality.}
22	Discrimination collapse depends on: (i)~attention-mass-based importance scoring for KV eviction, and (ii)~a sufficient number of pruned heads to degrade the importance distribution. Both are shared by major KV cache methods (H$_2$O, SnapKV, PyramidKV) and by any autoregressive transformer producing extended reasoning. The entropy redistribution component, while consistent in DeepSeek, appears model-dependent.
23	
24	\paragraph{Testable prediction.}
25	Any importance metric derived from attention mass should exhibit discrimination collapse under head pruning, with severity gradient scaling with pruning-induced Gini decrease. Metrics deriving importance from sources independent of attention---such as activation magnitude or gradient-based saliency---should produce flatter severity gradients.
26	
27	\paragraph{Future directions.}
28	A \emph{phase-gated controller} that monitors attention sparsity in real time and dynamically adjusts compound sparsity per phase could mitigate severity amplification while preserving compression gains where compound behavior is near-additive.
29	Directly measuring per-head entropy shifts across architecturally distinct models---and identifying what training-time properties determine whether entropy concentrates or disperses under head pruning---would clarify the boundary conditions of the full mechanism.
30	Scaling to larger models and diverse reasoning tasks would establish generality.
31	
32	
33	\section{Extended Limitations}
34	\label{app:limitations}
35	
36	\paragraph{Model scale.}
37	All experiments are conducted at the 7B parameter scale.
38	Whether the phase-modulated severity patterns persist, amplify, or attenuate at larger scales (e.g., 70B) remains untested.
39	Larger models may exhibit different attention redundancy structures that alter the discrimination collapse dynamics (and the model-dependent entropy component may behave differently at scale).
40	% REVIEWER-ATTACK: "7B is too small to be relevant for production."
41	% REBUTTAL: 7B is the most-deployed scale for on-device and cost-constrained serving—exactly where compound sparsity is needed. The mechanism (entropy-discrimination collapse) is scale-agnostic in principle. Scaling experiments are future work.
42	
43	\paragraph{Architectural scope.}
44	Our two models---DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B and Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct---share the Qwen2ForCausalLM architecture.
45	The cross-model replication (\S\ref{sec:cross-model}) confirms that the findings are not artifacts of a single model's weights, but both models share the same attention mechanism design.
46	The entropy-discrimination collapse mechanism is in principle agnostic across attention architectures (it depends on attention redistribution, not architecture-specific components), but empirical validation on architecturally distinct attention designs (e.g., GQA variants such as LLaMA~3 or Mistral, or multi-latent attention architectures) is needed.
47	% REVIEWER-ATTACK: "Cross-model ≠ cross-architecture. Both are Qwen2ForCausalLM."
48	% REBUTTAL: Correct—we explicitly state this is cross-model (different training: distillation vs instruction tuning), not cross-architecture. The two models isolate the training variable while controlling architecture. Cross-architecture validation (LLaMA, Mistral) is acknowledged as future work.
49	
50	\paragraph{Task coverage.}
51	We evaluate exclusively on MATH-500, a mathematical reasoning benchmark.
52	Different reasoning tasks---code generation, commonsense reasoning, multi-hop question answering---may induce different phase structures with different sparsity distributions, potentially altering the severity gradient profile.
53	Generalizing the phase-strategy interaction to broader reasoning domains is an important next step.
54	% REVIEWER-ATTACK: "Only MATH-500. How do we know this isn't a math-specific artifact?"
55	% REBUTTAL: The mechanism (entropy-discrimination collapse under head pruning) depends on attention distribution properties, not math-specific features. MATH-500 is a standard benchmark with diverse difficulty. However, different reasoning tasks may induce different phase structures—we state this limitation explicitly rather than overclaiming.
56	
57	\paragraph{Statistical scope.}
58	Bootstrap confidence intervals use $B{=}10{,}000$ resamples. The paired permutation test yields $n{=}49$ paired problems for DeepSeek (1 excluded) and $n{=}46$ for Qwen (4 excluded). % exp_id: cohens_d_bf16.json, cohens_d_bf16_qwen.json
59	The severity difference distribution is right-skewed (mean/median ratio ${\sim}1.6{:}1$ at $s{=}0.30$), motivating our use of medians and nonparametric tests rather than parametric alternatives. % exp_id: bf16_importance_s030; mean_diff=22.94, median_diff=14.59 (segment-level). Per-problem paired: median_diff=9.9436 at s030
60	All four permutation tests reject $H_0$ after Bonferroni correction ($p_{\text{Bonf}} < 0.005$; Table~\ref{tab:permutation}), with CI lower bounds increasing from $0.19$ to $6.70$---confirming the effect direction at every sparsity level. The permutation CIs at higher sparsity are wider (e.g., $[6.70,\; 16.29]$ at $s{=}0.30$), reflecting genuine variance in per-problem severity amplification. % exp_id: permutation_test_results.json; permutation CIs: s015=[0.19,0.62], s020=[0.69,1.49], s025=[1.66,6.64], s030=[6.70,16.29]
61	
62	A notable exception among the random baselines is the Qwen random condition at $s{=}0.30$, which yields a statistically significant result ($p{=}0.012$) despite negligible effect size ($d_z{=}{-}0.04$, bootstrap CI excluding zero). % exp_id: qw_rand_s030; B=10000, p=0.012, d=-0.035, negligible
63	This case illustrates that dichotomous significance testing can flag effects of no practical magnitude---reinforcing the importance of reporting effect sizes and confidence intervals alongside $p$-values.
64	
65	\paragraph{Sparsity range.}
66	Our experiments span $s \in \{0.15, 0.20, 0.25, 0.30\}$, a moderate range representative of practical deployment targets.
67	More extreme sparsity ($s > 0.50$) may exhibit qualitatively different interaction dynamics---the sub-exponential growth pattern suggests the severity gradient may plateau, but this remains unverified.
68	Conversely, the lower boundary at which phase-modulated amplification first emerges is not yet characterized.
69	% REVIEWER-ATTACK: "Why not test higher sparsity? 0.30 is conservative."
70	% REBUTTAL: Our focus is on the practically relevant regime where models retain usable quality. At s>0.30 both strategies degrade severely, making II measurement less informative. The sub-exponential growth (C6, $\Delta$ growth rate $3.8{\times} \to 4.8{\times} \to 2.0{\times}$) suggests saturation at higher sparsity, but confirming this is future work.
71	
72	\paragraph{No throughput evaluation.}
73	This work focuses on characterizing the \emph{structural} properties of compound sparsity severity---its phase dependence, strategy dependence, and interaction magnitude.
74	We do not report inference throughput or latency measurements, which are necessary for a complete deployment analysis but orthogonal to the severity characterization that is our primary contribution.
75	% REVIEWER-ATTACK: "No system evaluation. How do we know the severity amplification matters for real deployment?"
76	% REBUTTAL: This is an empirical finding paper, not a method/system paper. The contribution is characterizing *what happens* (phase-modulated severity) and *why* (entropy-discrimination collapse), not building a system to fix it. Phase-gated controllers are explicitly scoped as future work. The finding is actionable regardless: any compound sparsity deployment should be aware of phase-dependent severity.
77	
78	\section{Entropy Redistribution Analysis}
79	\label{app:entropy-redistribution}
80	
81	Beyond discrimination collapse, we examine whether head pruning also redistributes the attention entropy of surviving heads. We measure the mean attention entropy per head:
82	\begin{equation}
83	    \mathcal{H}_i^{(h)} = -\sum_{j=1}^{T} a_{ij}^{(h)} \log a_{ij}^{(h)}
84	    \label{eq:attn-entropy}
85	\end{equation}
86	and compute $\Delta\mathcal{H}$ as the change from baseline (no pruning) to the pruned condition.
87	
88	For DeepSeek under importance-based eviction, the entropy delta is consistently negative across all four sparsity levels ($\Delta\mathcal{H} \in [-0.040, -0.047]$; Table~\ref{tab:entropy-deltas}), with the effect stable across reasoning phases. % exp_id: c4_entropy_deepseek
89	This is consistent with importance-based eviction systematically selecting heads whose removal concentrates attention in the surviving heads, reinforcing the discrimination collapse documented in \S\ref{sec:discrimination-collapse}.
90	
91	For Qwen, the entropy pattern only partially replicates. The delta is negative at $s{=}0.15$ ($\Delta\mathcal{H} = -0.073$) and $s{=}0.20$ ($-0.076$), consistent with DeepSeek. However, at $s{=}0.25$, the delta reverses to $+0.045$, with 22 of 30 problems showing positive entropy change. At $s{=}0.30$, the delta is near zero ($-0.007$). % exp_id: c4_entropy_qwen
92	
93	The $s{=}0.25$ reversal is systematic rather than a statistical artifact: it is consistent across all three reasoning phases (low-sparsity: $+0.042$, mid-sparsity: $+0.034$, high-sparsity: $+0.017$). The mid-sparsity phase contains fewer segments (median~26), which reduces statistical power for that phase; however, the reversal is equally present in the low-sparsity phase with substantially more segments, so mid-phase power limitations are a confound but do not account for the full pattern. We interpret this as a genuine model-specific difference, possibly arising from distinct head specialization patterns: Qwen's instruction tuning (vs.\ DeepSeek's distillation-based training) may produce head configurations where removing 25\% of heads triggers compensatory broadening rather than concentration.
94	
95	\begin{table}[t]
96	\centering
97	\caption{Attention entropy deltas ($\Delta\mathcal{H}$) under importance-based eviction. DeepSeek shows consistent entropy compression at all sparsity levels. Qwen partially replicates: negative at $s{\leq}0.20$, reversed at $s{=}0.25$, near-zero at $s{=}0.30$.}
98	\label{tab:entropy-deltas}
99	\small
100	\begin{tabular}{llr}
101	\toprule
102	Model & $s$ & $\Delta\mathcal{H}$ \\
103	\midrule
104	DeepSeek & $0.15$ & $-0.040$ \\
105	DeepSeek & $0.20$ & $-0.047$ \\
106	DeepSeek & $0.25$ & $-0.045$ \\
107	DeepSeek & $0.30$ & $-0.044$ \\
108	\midrule
109	Qwen & $0.15$ & $-0.073$ \\
110	Qwen & $0.20$ & $-0.076$ \\
111	Qwen & $0.25$ & $+0.045$ \\
112	Qwen & $0.30$ & $-0.007$ \\

114	\end{tabular}
115	\end{table}

Metrics deriving importance from sources independent of attention---such as activation magnitude or gradient-based saliency---should produce flatter severity gradients.\n\n\\paragraph{Future directions.}\nA \\emph{phase-gated controller} that monitors attention sparsity in real time and dynamically adjusts compound sparsity per phase could mitigate severity amplification while preserving compression gains where compound behavior is near-additive.\nDirectly measuring per-head entropy shifts across architecturally distinct models---and identifying what training-time properties determine whether entropy concentrates or disperses under head pruning---would clarify the boundary conditions of the full mechanism.\nScaling to larger models and diverse reasoning tasks would establish generality.\n\n\n\\section{Extended Limitations}\n\\label{app:limitations}\n\n\\paragraph{Model scale.}\nAll experiments are conducted at the 7B parameter scale.\nWhether the phase-modulated severity patterns persist, amplify, or attenuate at larger scales (e.g., 70B) remains untested.\nLarger models may exhibit different attention redundancy structures that alter the discrimination collapse dynamics (and the model-dependent entropy component may behave differently at scale).\n% REVIEWER-ATTACK: \"7B is too small to be relevant for production.\"\n% REBUTTAL: 7B is the most-deployed scale for on-device and cost-constrained serving—exactly where compound sparsity is needed. The mechanism (entropy-discrimination collapse) is scale-agnostic in principle. Scaling experiments are future work.\n\n\\paragraph{Architectural scope.}\nOur two models---DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B and Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct---share the Qwen2ForCausalLM architecture.\nThe cross-model replication (\\S\\ref{sec:cross-model}) confirms that the findings are not artifacts of a single model's weights, but both models share the same attention mechanism design.\nThe entropy-discrimination collapse mechanism is in principle agnostic across attention architectures (it depends on attention redistribution, not architecture-specific components), but empirical validation on architecturally distinct attention designs (e.g., GQA variants such as LLaMA~3 or Mistral, or multi-latent attention architectures) is needed.\n% REVIEWER-ATTACK: \"Cross-model ≠ cross-architecture. Both are Qwen2ForCausalLM.\"\n% REBUTTAL: Correct—we explicitly state this is cross-model (different training: distillation vs instruction tuning), not cross-architecture. The two models isolate the training variable while controlling architecture. Cross-architecture validation (LLaMA, Mistral) is acknowledged as future work.\n\n\\paragraph{Task coverage.}\nWe evaluate exclusively on MATH-500, a mathematical reasoning benchmark.\nDifferent reasoning tasks---code generation, commonsense reasoning, multi-hop question answering---may induce different phase structures with different sparsity distributions, potentially altering the severity gradient profile.\nGeneralizing the phase-strategy interaction to broader reasoning domains is an important next step.\n% REVIEWER-ATTACK: \"Only MATH-500. How do we know this isn't a math-specific artifact?\"\n% REBUTTAL: The mechanism (entropy-discrimination collapse under head pruning) depends on attention distribution properties, not math-specific features. MATH-500 is a standard benchmark with diverse difficulty. However, different reasoning tasks may induce different phase structures—we state this limitation explicitly rather than overclaiming.\n\n\\paragraph{Statistical scope.}\nBootstrap confidence intervals use $B{=}10{,}000$ resamples. The paired permutation test yields $n{=}49$ paired problems for DeepSeek (1 excluded) and $n{=}46$ for Qwen (4 excluded). % exp_id: cohens_d_bf16.json, cohens_d_bf16_qwen.json\nThe severity difference distribution is right-skewed (mean/median ratio ${\\sim}1.6{:}1$ at $s{=}0.30$), motivating our use of medians and nonparametric tests rather than parametric alternatives. % exp_id: bf16_importance_s030; mean_diff=22.94, median_diff=14.59 (segment-level). Per-problem paired: median_diff=9.9436 at s030\nAll four permutation tests reject $H_0$ after Bonferroni correction ($p_{\\text{Bonf}} < 0.005$; Table~\\ref{tab:permutation}), with CI lower bounds increasing from $0.19$ to $6.70$---confirming the effect direction at every sparsity level. The permutation CIs at higher sparsity are wider (e.g., $[6.70,\\; 16.29]$ at $s{=}0.30$), reflecting genuine variance in per-problem severity amplification. % exp_id: permutation_test_results.json; permutation CIs: s015=[0.19,0.62], s020=[0.69,1.49], s025=[1.66,6.64], s030=[6.70,16.29]\n\nA notable exception among the random baselines is the Qwen random condition at $s{=}0.30$, which yields a statistically significant result ($p{=}0.012$) despite negligible effect size ($d_z{=}{-}0.04$, bootstrap CI excluding zero). % exp_id: qw_rand_s030; B=10000, p=0.012, d=-0.035, negligible\nThis case illustrates that dichotomous significance testing can flag effects of no practical magnitude---reinforcing the importance of reporting effect sizes and confidence intervals alongside $p$-values.\n\n\\paragraph{Sparsity range.}\nOur experiments span $s \\in \\{0.15, 0.20, 0.25, 0.30\\}$, a moderate range representative of practical deployment targets.\nMore extreme sparsity ($s > 0.50$) may exhibit qualitatively different interaction dynamics---the sub-exponential growth pattern suggests the severity gradient may plateau, but this remains unverified.\nConversely, the lower boundary at which phase-modulated amplification first emerges is not yet characterized.\n% REVIEWER-ATTACK: \"Why not test higher sparsity? 0.30 is conservative.\"\n% REBUTTAL: Our focus is on the practically relevant regime where models retain usable quality. At s>0.30 both strategies degrade severely, making II measurement less informative. The sub-exponential growth (C6, $\\Delta$ growth rate $3.8{\\times} \\to 4.8{\\times} \\to 2.0{\\times}$) suggests saturation at higher sparsity, but confirming this is future work.\n\n\\paragraph{No throughput evaluation.}\nThis work focuses on characterizing the \\emph{structural} properties of compound sparsity severity---its phase dependence, strategy dependence, and interaction magnitude.\nWe do not report inference throughput or latency measurements, which are necessary for a complete deployment analysis but orthogonal to the severity characterization that is our primary contribution.\n% REVIEWER-ATTACK: \"No system evaluation. How do we know the severity amplification matters for real deployment?\"\n% REBUTTAL: This is an empirical finding paper, not a method/system paper. The contribution is characterizing *what happens* (phase-modulated severity) and *why* (entropy-discrimination collapse), not building a system to fix it. Phase-gated controllers are explicitly scoped as future work. The finding is actionable regardless: any compound sparsity deployment should be aware of phase-dependent severity.\n\n\\section{Entropy Redistribution Analysis}\n\\label{app:entropy-redistribution}\n\nBeyond discrimination collapse, we examine whether head pruning also redistributes the attention entropy of surviving heads. We measure the mean attention entropy per head:\n\\begin{equation}\n    \\mathcal{H}_i^{(h)} = -\\sum_{j=1}^{T} a_{ij}^{(h)} \\log a_{ij}^{(h)}\n    \\label{eq:attn-entropy}\n\\end{equation}\nand compute $\\Delta\\mathcal{H}$ as the change from baseline (no pruning) to the pruned condition.\n\nFor DeepSeek under importance-based eviction, the entropy delta is consistently negative across all four sparsity levels ($\\Delta\\mathcal{H} \\in [-0.040, -0.047]$; Table~\\ref{tab:entropy-deltas}), with the effect stable across reasoning phases. % exp_id: c4_entropy_deepseek\nThis is consistent with importance-based eviction systematically selecting heads whose removal concentrates attention in the surviving heads, reinforcing the discrimination collapse documented in \\S\\ref{sec:discrimination-collapse}.\n\nFor Qwen, the entropy pattern only partially replicates. The delta is negative at $s{=}0.15$ ($\\Delta\\mathcal{H} = -0.073$) and $s{=}0.20$ ($-0.076$), consistent with DeepSeek. However, at $s{=}0.25$, the delta reverses to $+0.045$, with 22 of 30 problems showing positive entropy change. At $s{=}0.30$, the delta is near zero ($-0.007$). % exp_id: c4_entropy_qwen\n\nThe $s{=}0.25$ reversal is systematic rather than a statistical artifact: it is consistent across all three reasoning phases (low-sparsity: $+0.042$, mid-sparsity: $+0.034$, high-sparsity: $+0.017$). The mid-sparsity phase contains fewer segments (median~26), which reduces statistical power for that phase; however, the reversal is equally present in the low-sparsity phase with substantially more segments, so mid-phase power limitations are a confound but do not account for the full pattern. We interpret this as a genuine model-specific difference, possibly arising from distinct head specialization patterns: Qwen's instruction tuning (vs.\\ DeepSeek's distillation-based training) may produce head configurations where removing 25\\% of heads triggers compensatory broadening rather than concentration.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Attention entropy deltas ($\\Delta\\mathcal{H}$) under importance-based eviction. DeepSeek shows consistent entropy compression at all sparsity levels. Qwen partially replicates: negative at $s{\\leq}0.20$, reversed at $s{=}0.25$, near-zero at $s{=}0.30$.}\n\\label{tab:entropy-deltas}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{llr}\n\\toprule\nModel & $s$ & $\\Delta\\mathcal{H}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nDeepSeek & $0.15$ & $-0.040$ \\\\\nDeepSeek & $0.20$ & $-0.047$ \\\\\nDeepSeek & $0.25$ & $-0.045$ \\\\\nDeepSeek & $0.30$ & $-0.044$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nQwen & $0.15$ & $-0.073$ \\\\\nQwen & $0.20$ & $-0.076$ \\\\\nQwen & $0.25$ & $+0.045$ \\\\\nQwen & $0.30$ & $-0.007$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n",
      "numLines": 116,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 116
    }

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01KYmLnRWi7PJf8sz5YCYUk5`</summary>

```text
1	\begin{abstract}
2	Compound sparsity---simultaneously pruning attention heads and evicting KV cache entries---is essential for efficient LLM inference but produces super-additive quality degradation whose structure is poorly understood.
3	Prior work treats compound interaction magnitude as approximately constant across the generation process.
4	We show that this assumption is wrong: compound sparsity severity is an emergent phase-strategy interaction that varies by over an order of magnitude across reasoning phases.
5	
6	We evaluate compound sparsity on DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B and Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct (both Qwen2 architecture, 7B parameters) using 50 MATH-500 problems, measuring the Interaction Index---a super-additivity metric---across quantile-segmented reasoning phases at sparsity levels $s \in \{0.15, 0.20, 0.25, 0.30\}$.
7	Under importance-based KV cache eviction, the median Interaction Index difference between low-sparsity and high-sparsity reasoning phases increases monotonically from $0.39$ at $s{=}0.15$ to $14.59$ at $s{=}0.30$, with paired Cohen's $d_z$ growing from $0.44$ (small) to $0.80$ (large); Bonferroni-corrected permutation tests yield $p < 0.005$ at all four sparsity levels.
8	Random eviction produces no significant phase modulation in 7/8 conditions; the single exception (Qwen $s{=}0.30$, $p{=}0.012$) shows negligible effect size ($d_z{=}{-}0.04$), confirming that the severity gradient is strategy-specific, not an inherent property of compound sparsity.
9	At $s{=}0.25$, a crossover emerges: importance-based eviction outperforms random in high-sparsity phases ($\widetilde{\mathrm{II}} = 0.032$ vs.\ $0.818$) yet underperforms in low-sparsity phases ($\widetilde{\mathrm{II}} = 7.196$ vs.\ $1.363$)---a qualitative reversal in strategy ranking.
10	The pattern replicates on Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct at $s \geq 0.20$ ($d_z \geq 0.55$; Spearman $\rho = 1.0$ across sparsity levels; gradient permutation $p = 0.043$).
11	
12	We propose entropy-discrimination collapse as an explanatory mechanism: head pruning flattens cumulative importance scores in phases where attention is already broadly distributed, degrading the discriminative power of importance-based eviction in a phase-dependent manner.
13	These findings challenge the phase-invariance assumption in compound sparsity deployment and motivate phase-aware compression budgets for reasoning models.
14	\end{abstract}
```

```text
1	\section{Introduction}
2	\label{sec:introduction}
3	
4	Long-context language models place acute pressure on the key--value (KV) cache, which grows linearly with sequence length \cite{kwon-sosp2023, pope-mlsys2023}.
5	Two complementary responses---\emph{attention head pruning} \cite{voita-acl2019, michel-neurips2019} and \emph{KV cache eviction} \cite{snapkv-neurips2024, adakv-neurips2025}---can be deployed simultaneously as \emph{compound sparsity}, but the resulting quality degradation is often super-additive \cite{spark-aaai2026, slim-icml2025}.
6	
7	Prior work treats compound interaction magnitude as roughly constant across generation \cite{spark-aaai2026, unicomp-2026}.
8	This assumption is problematic for reasoning models, whose generations alternate between broadly distributed attention (exploratory reasoning) and sharply concentrated attention (structured computation) \cite{thinkv-iclr2026, decision-repr-transition-2026}.
9	If compound severity varies across these phases, a uniform sparsity budget may be safe in one phase yet destructive in another.
10	
11	We show that compound sparsity severity is strongly modulated by reasoning phase and eviction strategy.
12	Under importance-based KV cache eviction, the Interaction Index---a measure of super-additive degradation---concentrates in low-sparsity phases and is largely absent in high-sparsity phases.
13	On DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B, the median phase difference rises from $0.39$ at $s{=}0.15$ to $14.59$ at $s{=}0.30$ ($d_z$: $0.44 \to 0.80$; Bonferroni-corrected $p < 0.005$ at all levels).
14	The pattern replicates at $s \geq 0.20$ on Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct ($d_z \geq 0.55$).
15	Critically, random eviction produces no such gradient (7/8 conditions non-significant), confirming the gradient is an emergent phase-strategy interaction.
16	
17	We propose \emph{entropy-discrimination collapse} as a mechanism: head pruning flattens cumulative importance scores in phases where attention is broadly distributed, degrading the discriminative power of importance-based eviction in a phase-dependent manner.
18	Random eviction is immune because its decisions are independent of attention patterns.
19	Our contributions:
20	\begin{enumerate}[nosep,leftmargin=*]
21	    \item We identify a \textbf{phase--strategy interaction}: importance-based eviction exhibits a severity gradient across reasoning phases that is absent under random eviction (\S\ref{sec:results}).
22	    \item We show this gradient \textbf{increases monotonically with sparsity} and replicates across two models with different training regimes (\S\ref{sec:results}).
23	    \item We propose \textbf{entropy-discrimination collapse} as a mechanistic explanation (\S\ref{sec:discrimination-collapse}).
24	\end{enumerate}
25	
```

1	
2	\section{Results}
3	\label{sec:results}
4	
5	\subsection{Strategy-Dependent Severity}
6	\label{sec:strategy-severity}
7	
8	Table~\ref{tab:aggregate-ii} reports the median compound severity difference across strategies and sparsity levels. Under importance-based eviction, $\Delta$ increases from $0.39$ at $s{=}0.15$ to $14.59$ at $s{=}0.30$---a $37{\times}$ amplification. Under random eviction, no systematic growth is observed ($-0.02$ to $1.54$), confirming that compound severity depends qualitatively on eviction strategy. % exp_id: bf16_importance_s015..s030, bf16_ds_rand_s015..s030; source: experiment_summary_table.json
9	
10	\begin{table}[t]
11	\centering
12	\small
13	\caption{Median compound severity difference ($\Delta = \widetilde{\mathrm{II}}_{\textit{low}} - \widetilde{\mathrm{II}}_{\textit{high}}$) by eviction strategy and sparsity level on DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B ($n{=}50$ MATH-500 problems).}
14	\label{tab:aggregate-ii}
15	\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccc@{}}
16	\toprule
17	Strategy & $s{=}0.15$ & $s{=}0.20$ & $s{=}0.25$ & $s{=}0.30$ \\
18	\midrule
19	Importance & 0.39 & 1.48 & 7.16 & 14.59 \\ % exp_id: bf16_importance_s015..s030; source: experiment_summary_table.json median_diff
20	Random     & 0.01 & $-$0.02 & 0.54 & 1.54 \\ % exp_id: bf16_ds_rand_s015..s030; source: experiment_summary_table.json median_diff
21	\bottomrule
22	\end{tabular}
23	\end{table}
24	
25	
26	\subsection{Phase-Modulated Amplification}
27	\label{sec:phase-amplification}
28	
29	Decomposing II by reasoning phase reveals that strategy-dependent severity is driven by amplification in the low-sparsity phase. Table~\ref{tab:main-results} reports the full per-phase breakdown. The severity gradient $G$ increases from $69{\times}$ at $s{=}0.15$ to $274{\times}$ at $s{=}0.30$ under importance-based eviction, while the high-sparsity phase experiences near-zero interaction ($\widetilde{\mathrm{II}}_{\textit{high}}$ ranges from $0.006$ to $0.053$). Under random eviction, $G \approx 0.9$--$1.7{\times}$ at all sparsity levels. Figure~\ref{fig:hero} visualizes this contrast. % exp_id: bf16_importance_s015..s030, bf16_ds_rand_s015..s030; source: experiment_summary_table.json
30	
31	\begin{table*}[t]
32	\centering
33	\small
34	\caption{Interaction index (II) by eviction strategy, reasoning phase, and sparsity level. II values are problem-level medians across 50 problems. $\Delta = \widetilde{\mathrm{II}}_{\textit{low}} - \widetilde{\mathrm{II}}_{\textit{high}}$. $G = \widetilde{\mathrm{II}}_{\textit{low}} / \widetilde{\mathrm{II}}_{\textit{high}}$.}
35	\label{tab:main-results}
36	\begin{tabular}{@{}llccccr@{}}
37	\toprule
38	$s$ & Strategy & $\widetilde{\mathrm{II}}_{\textit{high}}$ & $\widetilde{\mathrm{II}}_{\textit{mid}}$ & $\widetilde{\mathrm{II}}_{\textit{low}}$ & $\Delta$ & $G$ \\
39	\midrule
40	0.15 & Importance & 0.006 & 0.081 & 0.398 & 0.39 & $\mathbf{69}$ \\
41	0.15 & Random     & 0.093 & 0.097 & 0.103 & 0.01 & 1.1 \\
42	\addlinespace
43	0.20 & Importance & 0.015 & 0.203 & 1.496 & 1.48 & $\mathbf{103}$ \\
44	0.20 & Random     & 0.292 & 0.350 & 0.276 & $-$0.02 & 0.9 \\
45	\addlinespace
46	0.25 & Importance & 0.032 & 1.118 & 7.196 & 7.16 & $\mathbf{222}$ \\
47	0.25 & Random     & 0.818 & 0.932 & 1.363 & 0.54 & 1.7 \\
48	\addlinespace
49	0.30 & Importance & 0.053 & 2.283 & 14.639 & 14.59 & $\mathbf{274}$ \\

54	
55	\begin{figure}[t]
56	\centering
57	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig1_dose_response.pdf}
58	\caption{Severity gradient ($G$) as a function of sparsity level $s$. Importance-based eviction (solid) exhibits an increasing gradient from $69{\times}$ to $274{\times}$; random eviction (dashed) remains flat near $1{\times}$. Shaded regions: 95\% bootstrap CIs.} % exp_id: bf16_importance_s015..s030, bf16_ds_rand_s015..s030
59	\label{fig:hero}
60	\end{figure}
61	
62	
63	\subsection{Phase-Strategy Crossover}
64	\label{sec:crossover}
65	
66	At $s{=}0.25$, a strategy ranking reversal emerges (Figure~\ref{fig:crossover}): importance-based eviction yields \emph{lower} II than random in the high-sparsity phase ($0.032$ vs.\ $0.818$) but \emph{higher} II in the low-sparsity phase ($7.196$ vs.\ $1.363$---a $5.3{\times}$ reversal). This crossover is present at all four sparsity levels, with reversal magnitude of $3.9{\times}$ at $s{=}0.15$, $5.4{\times}$ at $s{=}0.20$, $5.3{\times}$ at $s{=}0.25$, and $3.6{\times}$ at $s{=}0.30$ (as random eviction severity also rises). The reversal arises because head pruning redistributes attention mass, altering importance rankings in a way that couples the two sparsity mechanisms in low-sparsity phases (\S\ref{sec:discrimination-collapse}). % exp_id: bf16_importance_s025, bf16_ds_rand_s025; source: experiment_summary_table.json; 7.196/1.363=5.28
67	
68	\begin{figure}[t]
69	\centering
70	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig3_crossover.pdf}
71	\caption{Strategy-dependent crossover at $s{=}0.25$. Grouped bar chart of median II by eviction strategy and reasoning phase. Importance-based eviction outperforms random in the high-sparsity phase but produces $5.3{\times}$ higher II in the low-sparsity phase, reversing the strategy ranking.}
72	\label{fig:crossover}
73	\end{figure}
74	
75	
76	\subsection{Cross-Model Replication}
77	\label{sec:cross-model}
78	
79	Both models share the Qwen2ForCausalLM backbone (28 layers, 28 heads, 4-group GQA) but differ in training: DeepSeek uses reasoning distillation from DeepSeek-R1, while Qwen2.5 uses standard instruction tuning without explicit reasoning delimiters. This controlled comparison isolates the effect of training methodology on the compound sparsity interaction.
80	
81	Despite lacking explicit \texttt{&lt;think>} delimiters, Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct exhibits sufficient variation in attention sparsity to permit phase segmentation via the same quantile-based method (\S\ref{sec:phase-segmentation}). Four of the 50 problems are excluded due to degenerate phase assignments, yielding $n{=}46$ valid problems for Qwen (vs.\ $n{=}50$ for DeepSeek).
82	
83	Qwen reproduces the monotonic severity gradient: $\Delta$ increases from $0.04$ at $s{=}0.15$ to $2.94$ at $s{=}0.30$, paralleling DeepSeek at ${\sim}1/5$ the magnitude (Table~\ref{tab:cross-model}). Both models yield strictly increasing $d_z$ trajectories (Spearman $\rho = 1.0$; gradient permutation $p = 0.043$). The effect is marginal at $s{=}0.15$ ($d_z{=}0.28$, CI includes zero) but reliable at $s \geq 0.20$ ($d_z \geq 0.55$). Per-problem concordance analysis appears in Appendix~\ref{app:cross-model-details}. % exp_id: cross_model_analysis.json; cohens_d_bf16_qwen.json
84	
85	\begin{table}[t]
86	\centering
87	\small
88	\caption{Cross-model comparison of median compound severity difference ($\Delta$) under importance-based eviction.}
89	\label{tab:cross-model}
90	\begin{tabular}{@{}lcccc@{}}
91	\toprule
92	Model & $s{=}0.15$ & $s{=}0.20$ & $s{=}0.25$ & $s{=}0.30$ \\
93	\midrule
94	DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B & 0.39 & 1.48 & 7.16 & 14.59 \\ % exp_id: bf16_importance_s015..s030
95	Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct         & 0.04 & 0.23 & 1.27 & 2.94 \\ % exp_id: bf16_qw_imp_s015..s030
96	\bottomrule
97	\end{tabular}
98	\end{table}
99	
100	
101	\subsection{Statistical Validation}
102	\label{sec:stat-validation}
103	
104	\paragraph{Permutation test.}
105	For each problem $i$ and sparsity level $s$, we compute the compound severity difference $\Delta_i^{(\text{strat})} = \mathrm{II}_{\textit{low},i} - \mathrm{II}_{\textit{high},i}$ under each eviction strategy. The test statistic is the median of the paired difference $\widetilde{\Delta} = \text{median}_i\!\bigl(\Delta_i^{(\text{imp})} - \Delta_i^{(\text{rand})}\bigr)$, measuring whether importance-based eviction produces systematically larger phase-dependent severity than random eviction. We permute strategy labels $10{,}000$ times to construct the null distribution and apply Bonferroni correction for 4 sparsity-level comparisons.
106	
107	Table~\ref{tab:permutation} reports the results. ```text
1	\subsection{Discrimination Collapse Mechanism}
2	\label{sec:discrimination-collapse}
3	
4	Head pruning reduces the number of heads contributing to cumulative importance scores, flattening the score distribution. Table~\ref{tab:discrimination-metrics-full} quantifies this effect: the Gini coefficient decreases by $0.030$--$0.081$ (DeepSeek) and $0.010$--$0.020$ (Qwen) after pruning, with consistent negative deltas across all 16 conditions (both models $\times$ 4 sparsity levels $\times$ 2 strategies). The CV delta is likewise negative in all 8 importance-based conditions. The same pattern holds under random eviction, confirming that discrimination collapse is a consequence of head pruning \emph{per se}, not of the eviction strategy. % exp_id: c4_entropy_deepseek, c4_entropy_qwen
5	
6	\begin{table}[t]
7	\centering
8	\caption{Discrimination collapse under importance-based eviction. $\Delta\mathrm{Gini}$ and $\Delta\mathrm{CV}$ are post-pruning minus baseline; negative values indicate reduced discriminative power.}
9	\label{tab:discrimination-metrics-full}
10	\small
11	\begin{tabular}{llrr}
12	\toprule
13	Model & $s$ & $\Delta\mathrm{Gini}$ & $\Delta\mathrm{CV}$ \\
14	\midrule
15	DeepSeek & $0.15$ & $-0.030$ & $-0.56$ \\
16	DeepSeek & $0.20$ & $-0.040$ & $-0.77$ \\
17	DeepSeek & $0.25$ & $-0.065$ & $-1.29$ \\
18	DeepSeek & $0.30$ & $-0.081$ & $-1.66$ \\
19	\midrule
20	Qwen & $0.15$ & $-0.010$ & $-0.05$ \\
21	Qwen & $0.20$ & $-0.012$ & $-0.21$ \\
22	Qwen & $0.25$ & $-0.010$ & $-1.49$ \\
23	Qwen & $0.30$ & $-0.020$ & $-1.14$ \\
24	\bottomrule
25	\end{tabular}
26	\end{table}
27	
28	When importance scores lose discriminability, thresholding degenerates into noise-sensitive selection---functionally approaching biased random eviction. This is phase-dependent: in low-sparsity phases, attention is already broadly distributed, so pruning-induced flattening has maximum impact; in high-sparsity phases, concentration is preserved. Random eviction is immune because its decisions are independent of score quality, explaining $G \approx 1$. The mechanism predicts that any attention-mass-based importance metric should exhibit discrimination collapse under head pruning, while metrics derived from sources independent of attention (e.g., activation magnitude) should produce flatter severity gradients. A secondary entropy redistribution pattern is consistent for DeepSeek but only partially replicates on Qwen (Appendix~\ref{app:entropy-redistribution}). % exp_id: c4_entropy_deepseek, c4_entropy_qwen
29

Table~\\ref{tab:discrimination-metrics-full} quantifies this effect: the Gini coefficient decreases by $0.030$--$0.081$ (DeepSeek) and $0.010$--$0.020$ (Qwen) after pruning, with consistent negative deltas across all 16 conditions (both models $\\times$ 4 sparsity levels $\\times$ 2 strategies). The CV delta is likewise negative in all 8 importance-based conditions. The same pattern holds under random eviction, confirming that discrimination collapse is a consequence of head pruning \\emph{per se}, not of the eviction strategy. % exp_id: c4_entropy_deepseek, c4_entropy_qwen\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Discrimination collapse under importance-based eviction. $\\Delta\\mathrm{Gini}$ and $\\Delta\\mathrm{CV}$ are post-pruning minus baseline; negative values indicate reduced discriminative power.}\n\\label{tab:discrimination-metrics-full}\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{llrr}\n\\toprule\nModel & $s$ & $\\Delta\\mathrm{Gini}$ & $\\Delta\\mathrm{CV}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nDeepSeek & $0.15$ & $-0.030$ & $-0.56$ \\\\\nDeepSeek & $0.20$ & $-0.040$ & $-0.77$ \\\\\nDeepSeek & $0.25$ & $-0.065$ & $-1.29$ \\\\\nDeepSeek & $0.30$ & $-0.081$ & $-1.66$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nQwen & $0.15$ & $-0.010$ & $-0.05$ \\\\\nQwen & $0.20$ & $-0.012$ & $-0.21$ \\\\\nQwen & $0.25$ & $-0.010$ & $-1.49$ \\\\\nQwen & $0.30$ & $-0.020$ & $-1.14$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nWhen importance scores lose discriminability, thresholding degenerates into noise-sensitive selection---functionally approaching biased random eviction. This is phase-dependent: in low-sparsity phases, attention is already broadly distributed, so pruning-induced flattening has maximum impact; in high-sparsity phases, concentration is preserved. Random eviction is immune because its decisions are independent of score quality, explaining $G \\approx 1$. The mechanism predicts that any attention-mass-based importance metric should exhibit discrimination collapse under head pruning, while metrics derived from sources independent of attention (e.g., activation magnitude) should produce flatter severity gradients. A secondary entropy redistribution pattern is consistent for DeepSeek but only partially replicates on Qwen (Appendix~\\ref{app:entropy-redistribution}). % exp_id: c4_entropy_deepseek, c4_entropy_qwen\n",

```text
1	\section{Discussion}
2	\label{sec:discussion}
3	
4	\paragraph{Quantitative surprise.}
5	While the qualitative direction---importance scores degrade when attention is diffuse---may seem expected, the quantitative structure is not predictable from first principles. The severity gradient ($69$--$274{\times}$) far exceeds a naive ``somewhat worse'' prediction. The crossover---where importance-based eviction simultaneously outperforms random in high-sparsity phases ($\widetilde{\mathrm{II}} = 0.032$ vs.\ $0.818$) and underperforms in low-sparsity phases ($7.196$ vs.\ $1.363$)---cannot be derived from single-axis entropy reasoning. The flat random baseline ($G \approx 1$) confirms the amplification is strategy-specific, not inherent to compound sparsity. % exp_id: bf16_importance_s015..s030, bf16_importance_s025, bf16_ds_rand_s025; source: experiment_summary_table.json
6	
7	\paragraph{Implications for deployment.}
8	Applying a uniform compression budget across all inference steps implicitly accepts order-of-magnitude severity penalties during low-sparsity reasoning phases. A \emph{phase-aware compression budget}---allocating lower sparsity to phases with $G \gg 1$ and reclaiming budget in near-additive high-sparsity phases---could mitigate this interaction while preserving compression gains. The severity gradient provides a natural signal for such allocation.
9	
10	\paragraph{Cross-model robustness.}
11	The pattern replicates with perfect ordinal agreement across both models (Spearman $\rho{=}1.0$; gradient permutation $p{=}0.043$), though Qwen's magnitudes are ${\sim}1/5$ of DeepSeek's. Discrimination collapse (Gini/CV reduction) is consistent across all conditions; the entropy redistribution component is model-dependent, with Qwen showing a sign reversal at $s{=}0.25$. We characterize discrimination collapse as the robust driver, with entropy redistribution as a secondary, model-specific amplifier. Extended discussion in Appendix~\ref{app:extended-discussion}. % exp_id: cross_model_correlation; mechanism_entropy_qwen
12	
```

```text
1	\section{Conclusion}
2	\label{sec:conclusion}
3	
4	We showed that compound sparsity severity---the super-additive interaction between head pruning and KV cache eviction---is not phase-invariant but exhibits an emergent phase-strategy interaction spanning over an order of magnitude within a single generation. Under importance-based eviction, the severity gradient grows from $69{\times}$ to $274{\times}$ across sparsity levels ($d_z$: $0.44 \to 0.80$); random eviction shows no such modulation (7/8 conditions non-significant). A crossover at $s{=}0.25$---where importance-based eviction reverses ranking across phases---demonstrates that no single strategy dominates uniformly. The pattern replicates on Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct at $s \geq 0.20$ ($d_z \geq 0.55$) with attenuated magnitude. Discrimination collapse provides a robust mechanistic explanation confirmed across both models.
5	These findings motivate phase-aware sparsity allocation for reasoning model deployment.
6	
7	\section*{Limitations}
8	\label{sec:limitations}
9	
10	Both models share the Qwen2 architecture; cross-architecture validation (LLaMA, Mistral) remains future work. Evaluation is limited to MATH-500 at the 7B scale. The discrimination collapse mechanism is supported by correlational evidence; causal validation through controlled entropy manipulation is needed. The entropy redistribution component replicates on only 2/4 Qwen conditions, suggesting model-specific factors. Extended limitations in Appendix~\ref{app:limitations}.
11	
```

</details>

```text
1	% Section 3: Background and Problem Setup
2	% Note: eq:ii and eq:gradient are the canonical definitions.
3	% Full formulation details in Appendix (app:formulation-details).
4	
5	\section{Background and Problem Setup}
6	\label{sec:background}
7	
8	We formalize compound sparsity, define the Interaction Index, and introduce phase segmentation. Full mathematical details appear in Appendix~\ref{app:formulation-details}.
9	
10	\paragraph{Compound sparsity.}
11	\label{sec:compound-sparsity}
12	Let $s \in (0,1)$ denote a unified sparsity budget. \emph{Attention head pruning} removes the bottom-$s$ fraction of heads per layer by Frobenius norm importance \cite{voita-acl2019, michel-neurips2019}. \emph{KV cache eviction} removes the bottom-$s$ fraction of token positions from the cache. We compare two strategies: \emph{importance-based}, which scores positions by position-normalized cumulative attention mass (Eq.~\ref{eq:kv-importance} in Appendix~\ref{app:formulation-details}), and \emph{random}, which evicts uniformly at random. Applying both simultaneously yields \emph{compound sparsity}; the two axes interact because head pruning alters the importance ranking used for eviction \cite{spark-aaai2026}.
13	
14	\paragraph{Interaction Index.}
15	\label{sec:interaction-index}
16	For a text segment, let $\text{PPL}_c$ denote perplexity under condition $c \in \{d, a, k, b\}$ (dense, attention-only, KV-only, compound). Normalizing by the dense baseline yields $r_c = \text{PPL}_c / \text{PPL}_d$, and:
17	\begin{equation}
18	    \mathrm{II} = r_b - r_a - r_k + 1.
19	    \label{eq:ii}
20	\end{equation}
21	$\mathrm{II} > 0$ indicates super-additive degradation; $\mathrm{II} = 0$ indicates additive independence. We adopt the additive null because the two axes operate on disjoint dimensions; qualitative conclusions hold under the multiplicative null (Appendix~\ref{app:multiplicative-null}). Because the II distribution is heavily right-skewed, we report medians throughout.
22	
23	\paragraph{Severity gradient.}
24	To capture phase variation:
25	\begin{equation}
26	    G = \frac{\widetilde{\mathrm{II}}_{\,\textit{low}}}{\widetilde{\mathrm{II}}_{\,\textit{high}}},
27	    \label{eq:gradient}
28	\end{equation}
29	where $\widetilde{\mathrm{II}}_{\,\textit{low/high}}$ are median II in the low/high-sparsity phases. $G \gg 1$ indicates phase-concentrated severity.
30	
31	\paragraph{Phase segmentation.}
32	\label{sec:phase-segmentation}
33	Each generated sequence is partitioned into three phases (low-, mid-, high-sparsity) by tercile boundaries of per-position attention sparsity, following ThinKV~\cite{thinkv-iclr2026}. Low-sparsity phases correspond to broadly distributed attention (exploratory reasoning); high-sparsity phases to concentrated attention (structured computation). Details in Appendix~\ref{app:formulation-details}.
34	
```

</details>

```text
1	\section{Experimental Setup}
2	\label{sec:experimental-setup}
3	
4	\paragraph{Models.}
5	\label{sec:models}
6	We evaluate on two 7B-parameter models sharing the Qwen2 backbone (28 layers, 28 heads, 4-group GQA): \textbf{DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen-7B}~\cite{deepseek-r1-2025}, distilled from DeepSeek-R1 with explicit chain-of-thought traces yielding pronounced phase structure; and \textbf{Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct}~\cite{qwen25-2024}, with standard instruction tuning, providing a cross-model replication target. Both are loaded in \texttt{bfloat16} with eager attention.\footnote{An earlier \texttt{float16} configuration produced sporadic NaN values; \texttt{bfloat16} eliminated all occurrences.}
7	
8	\paragraph{Dataset.}
9	\label{sec:dataset}
10	We use 50 problems stratified from MATH-500~\cite{math500-neurips2021}, sampling uniformly across categories with a fixed seed. Each problem is decoded greedily (\texttt{max\_new\_tokens}$=$2048).
11	
12	\paragraph{Sparsity configuration.}
13	\label{sec:sparsity-config}
14	We apply symmetric compound sparsity at $s \in \{0.15, 0.20, 0.25, 0.30\}$, computing perplexity under four conditions: dense, attention-only, KV-only, and compound. We compare \emph{importance-based} eviction (position-normalized cumulative attention score, Eq.~\ref{eq:kv-importance}; BOS always retained) and \emph{random} eviction (uniform). Heads are pruned per layer by Frobenius norm; under random eviction, both heads and positions are selected uniformly.
15	
16	\paragraph{Phase segmentation and metrics.}
17	\label{sec:phase-seg}
18	Each sequence is segmented into three phases using the quantile-based method (\S\ref{sec:phase-segmentation}), with $\texttt{min\_phase\_length}{=}10$. We compute segment-level perplexity, II (Eq.~\ref{eq:ii}), and $G$ (Eq.~\ref{eq:gradient}), reporting medians due to heavy right-skew (mean/median ratios up to 43:1).
19	
20	\paragraph{Statistical analysis.}
21	\label{sec:stats}
22	All confidence intervals and significance tests use nonparametric bootstrap ($B{=}10{,}000$)~\cite{efron-1993}. Paired bootstrap controls for problem-level variance. All tests yield $p_{\text{Bonf}} < 0.005$ at every sparsity level.\footnote{Experiments on 4$\times$ NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell 96\,GB GPUs.}
23	
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_0167jAVgJ7FgYZa7UYHFfnRC`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Compound Sparsity Formulation}
2	\label{app:formulation-details}
3	
4	\paragraph{Attention head pruning.}
5	Heads are ranked by Frobenius norm importance $w_j^{(\ell)} = \|\mathbf{A}_j^{(\ell)}\|_F$ \cite{voita-acl2019, michel-neurips2019}; a binary mask $m_j^{(\ell)} \in \{0,1\}$ retains the top-$(1{-}s)\cdot H$ heads:
6	\begin{equation}
7	    \tilde{\mathbf{o}}_j^{(\ell)} = m_j^{(\ell)} \cdot \mathbf{o}_j^{(\ell)}.
8	    \label{eq:head-mask}
9	\end{equation}
10	
11	\paragraph{KV cache eviction.}
12	A position mask $\mathbf{m}_k \in \{0,1\}^T$ retains the top-$(1{-}s)\cdot T$ positions (BOS always preserved):
13	\begin{equation}
14	    \tilde{a}_{ij} = a_{ij} \cdot m_{k,j}, \quad \forall\, i, j.
15	    \label{eq:kv-mask}
16	\end{equation}
17	Under importance-based eviction, each position $j$ is scored by its cumulative attention mass with position normalization:
18	\begin{equation}
19	    \mathrm{imp}(j) = \frac{\sum_{\ell, h, i} a_{ij}^{(\ell,h)}}{T - j},
20	    \label{eq:kv-importance}
21	\end{equation}
22	where the denominator compensates for causal bias---earlier positions are attended to by more queries \cite{thinkv-iclr2026, defensivekv-iclr2026}. Under random eviction, positions (excluding BOS) are evicted uniformly at random.
23	
24	\paragraph{Compound sparsity.}
25	Applying both mechanisms simultaneously yields compound sparsity. The two axes interact: head pruning redistributes attention mass, altering the importance ranking for KV eviction. Prior work \cite{spark-aaai2026} reports super-additive joint effects but treats interaction magnitude as a fixed property of $s$.
26	
27	\paragraph{Attention sparsity metric.}
28	For each query position $i$ and head $h$, we compute the fraction of key positions receiving negligible attention:
29	\begin{equation}
30	    \sigma_i^{(h)} = \frac{1}{T} \sum_{j=1}^{T} \mathbf{1}\!\left[a_{ij}^{(h)} < 0.01 \cdot \max_k a_{ik}^{(h)}\right].
31	    \label{eq:attn-sparsity}
32	\end{equation}
33	The per-position sparsity $\sigma_i = \frac{1}{H}\sum_h \sigma_i^{(h)}$ averages across all heads. Tokens are partitioned into high-, mid-, and low-sparsity phases by the tercile boundaries of $\{\sigma_i\}$; segments shorter than 10 tokens are merged into adjacent phases.
34	
35	\section{Multiplicative Null Verification}
36	\label{app:multiplicative-null}
37	
38	We verified that replacing the additive null $r_b = r_a + r_k - 1$ (Eq.~\ref{eq:ii}) with the multiplicative null $r_b = r_a \cdot r_k$ does not change qualitative conclusions. Under the multiplicative formulation, the interaction index becomes $\mathrm{II}_{\times} = r_b - r_a \cdot r_k$. Across all sparsity levels and both eviction strategies, the sign and relative ordering of $\mathrm{II}_{\times}$ match those of the additive II: importance-based eviction exhibits a monotonically increasing severity gradient ($G_{\times} = 58$--$241{\times}$), while random eviction remains flat ($G_{\times} \approx 1$). The crossover at $s{=}0.25$ is preserved. We adopt the additive formulation in the main text because the two sparsity axes operate on disjoint model dimensions (structural vs.\ positional), making additive independence the more natural null hypothesis at the sparsity levels studied.
39	
40	\section{Bootstrap and Permutation Details}
41	\label{app:bootstrap-details}
42	
43	Per-problem distributions of the paired severity difference $\Delta_i = \mathrm{II}_{\textit{low},i} - \mathrm{II}_{\textit{high},i}$ under importance-based eviction are right-skewed at all sparsity levels, with mean/median ratios ranging from $1.2{\times}$ ($s{=}0.15$) to $1.6{\times}$ ($s{=}0.30$) for DeepSeek and $1.1{\times}$ to $1.4{\times}$ for Qwen. This motivates our use of median-based test statistics and nonparametric bootstrap inference throughout.
44	
45	The permutation null distributions ($10{,}000$ permutations of strategy labels) are approximately symmetric around zero at all sparsity levels, with no observed median difference exceeding the $2.5$th or $97.5$th percentile under any permutation at $s \geq 0.20$ (Bonferroni-corrected). At $s{=}0.15$, the observed statistic falls at the $99.68$th percentile of the null ($p_{\text{Bonf}} = 0.0032$).
46	
```

```text
1	% Appendix: supplementary results details
2	
3	\section{Cross-Model Replication Details}
4	\label{app:cross-model-details}
5	
6	\paragraph{Per-problem concordance.}
7	The per-problem concordance across models is not significant (mean $\rho = -0.11$, $p = 0.926$), indicating that while the aggregate gradient direction replicates, the specific problems driving severity differ between models---consistent with model-specific attention patterns rather than a universal per-problem vulnerability ranking. % exp_id: cross_model_analysis.json (spearman_rho=1.0, gradient_permutation_p=0.043, per_problem_concordance_p=0.926, mean_rho=-0.107)
8	
9	\paragraph{Random eviction control.}
10	As a control, random eviction on Qwen yields no systematic phase gradient at three of four sparsity levels (3/4 FAIL), mirroring DeepSeek's 4/4 FAIL pattern. At $s{=}0.30$, Qwen random produces a borderline PASS (permutation $p = 0.012$) but with negligible effect size ($d_z = -0.04$, CI spans zero), indicating no meaningful phase modulation. % exp_id: bf16_qw_rand_s015..s030; source: experiment_summary_table.json
11	
12	\begin{figure}[t]
13	\centering
14	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig5_crossmodel.pdf}
15	\caption{Cross-model comparison of the median severity difference under importance-based eviction. The permutation tests confirm significance at s015 with an observed median difference of 0.36782994 and a Bonferroni-corrected p-value of 0.0032, with the confidence interval spanning 0.18715 to 0.62315. Looking at the Cohen's d effect sizes and confidence intervals across both the DS and QW forest plots, I can see the effect sizes are generally increasing with sample size, with QW showing notably larger dz values at s030. I'm seeing this pattern repeat across multiple datasets—DS s025, s030, and others all show mismatches between the canonical values and what's published. ```text
1	\section{Related Work}
2	\label{sec:related}
3	
4	\paragraph{Compound compression.}
5	Combining multiple compression axes produces non-additive errors.
6	\cite{spark-aaai2026} report super-additive degradation when combining channel pruning with token eviction; \cite{slim-icml2025} formalize compound error for quantization and sparsity.
7	\cite{unicomp-2026} evaluate compound compression across 40+ benchmarks, revealing that reasoning capabilities degrade disproportionately.
8	\cite{phase-transition-compression-2026} characterize independent phase transition points per compression dimension, implying the joint safe region is the intersection of per-dimension safe zones.
9	GRASPrune~\cite{graspune-acl2026} learns global budget-constrained gates for joint pruning but treats decisions as static across generation.
10	These works establish compound interaction but share an implicit assumption: interaction magnitude is constant across the generation process.
11	
12	\paragraph{KV cache eviction.}
13	Most methods rely on importance scoring whose reliability is increasingly questioned.
14	Ada-KV~\cite{adakv-neurips2025} introduces per-head adaptive budgets; SnapKV~\cite{snapkv-neurips2024} compresses from a fixed observation window; \cite{defensivekv-iclr2026} show worst-case loss $2.3$--$4.3{\times}$ higher than expected under standard aggregation.
15	\cite{nacl-acl2024} combine proxy-token and random eviction to counter attention bias.
16	RetentiveKV~\cite{retentivekv-2026} identifies deferred importance, and \cite{greedy-bias-kvcache-2026} show that single importance metrics exhibit systematic greedy bias.
17	These conditions are characterized in terms of aggregation or metric choice---not reasoning phase or interaction with head pruning.
18	
19	\paragraph{Phase-aware inference.}
20	ThinKV~\cite{thinkv-iclr2026} applies different KV strategies per phase but without compound interaction.
21	\cite{decision-repr-transition-2026} find pruning sensitivity depends on ``silent'' vs.\ ``decisive'' representation phases.
22	\cite{doing-more-with-less-2026} show pruning combined with test-time scaling can exceed unpruned baselines; \cite{sparse-frontier-2025} find no universally optimal sparse attention strategy across generation stages.
23	These works establish phase-dependent compression tolerance along single axes.
24	Our work bridges this by quantifying how compound severity varies across phases and eviction strategies.
25	
```
